Abstract Loop-PP schedules cyclic pipeline stages for depth-recurrent models, overlapping communication and compute while preserving exact numerical equivalence, reducing memory and increasing throughput on MoE models.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Depth-recurrent (Looped) models raise effective depth by applying a shared stack of blocks several times. Scaling them usually requires pipeline parallelism, but commonly used interleaved 1F1B is drawn for an acyclic stage graph in which each micro-batch enters each stage once. Once that stack is split across stages, the same stage is visited several times on one micro-batch and later stages feed earlier ones: native 1F1B has no slot for the extra visit, cannot rank a first iteration against a second, and its neighbour-only send/receive order has no back edge to the first stage, so the arrangement stalls or fires out of order. We present Loop-PP, which rebuilds both the timetable and the communication for this cyclic layout. It schedules different iterations of the same stage separately, specializes a 1F1B skeleton into a globally consistent order over those visits, and emits a static instruction stream per rank that separates posting from waiting so communication overlaps local compute. Loop-PP is bit-identical to a single-stage reference. On 8B-60B MoE models, it lowers peak memory by as much as 32.8\%, and with compute budget fixed, the best observed throughput rises by 12.7\%.

Abstract GameDojo is a controllable benchmark of 120 boss encounters across 20 games with 600 hours of gameplay trajectories, showing frontier models struggle in real-time interaction while training GameDojo-4B achieves frontier performance and policy transfer across bosses.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Interactive intelligence requires agents to act on observations and adapt to the consequences of actions. Studying these abilities calls for diverse, challenging tasks under controlled conditions. We introduce GameDojo, a controllable interactive gameplay benchmark of 120 boss encounters across 20 games for evaluating model capabilities, learning algorithms, and agent harnesses. Coding agents reconstruct the environments and write gameplay scripts, producing GameDojo-Data with 600 hours of gameplay trajectories. Experiments reveal substantial challenges for frontier models, especially in real-time interaction. Training on GameDojo-Data brings GameDojo-4B to frontier performance, with the strongest real-time performance. We also find joint training improves individual tasks, and learned policies transfer across bosses. Together, these findings establish GameDojo as a challenging and valuable interactive gameplay benchmark for the community. Our project page is at https://gamedojo-benchmark.github.io/ , and our code is at https://github.com/THUSI-Lab/GameDojo .

Abstract A dual-agent framework with hierarchical memory and recursive self-improvement enables robotic manipulation agents to convert execution evidence into reusable guidance without updating model parameters.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Vision-language model (VLM)-based agents offer a promising approach to robotic manipulation, yet remain limited by inadequate memory organization, uncertain failure attribution, and limited reuse of execution experience. We propose Embodied-Harness, a dual-agent framework that improves manipulation by refining the execution harness without updating model parameters. The Actor interacts with the environment through a robot-control interface, while the Reflector converts execution evidence into reusable operational guidance. Hierarchical Memory Architecture maintains short-term execution context and long-term experiential knowledge to support execution and reflection. Evidence-based Experience Reflection grounds failure diagnosis in observed interactions, while Recursive Harness Self-Improvement refines operational guidance across successive attempts. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0, RoboSPA, and RMBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Embodied-Harness in generalization under environmental variations, spatial reasoning, long-horizon planning, and memory-dependent manipulation. Our method achieves success rates of 82.4%, 76.7%, and 94.4% on the three benchmarks, respectively. Real-world evaluation further yields an average success rate of 96.7% across three manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of Embodied-Harness to improve robotic manipulation through experience-driven harness self-improvement.

Abstract LoopRes enables pretrained large language models to benefit from looped computation by letting layers attend to recent earlier loops with trainable loop-aware keys, yielding consistent gains and improved test-time scaling.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.

Abstract  Recent multimodal search agents use reasoning, vision, and live web search across many turns to answer questions beyond parametric knowledge by orchestrating a small library of hand-crafted search and visual tools. However, the library is laborious to create and maintain, and frozen when new query types, evidence formats, and failure modes are encountered in the wild. Human practitioners, by contrast, cope with insufficient tools by fashioning new ones from familiar parts and reusing them across tasks. Inspired by this, we propose ToolSmith, an automatic tool-generation framework that grows and reuses modules on top of a lean base tool library. We evaluate ToolSmith on three in-domain multimodal search benchmarks and ten out-of-domain benchmarks under a VLM-as-a-Judge protocol. ToolSmith consistently improves over the base library across deployed agents of varying scales, with larger backbones reaping greater gains, demonstrating the scalability of the approach. Our code and data are available at https://threesr.github.io/toolsmith/.

Abstract WorldLatent unifies 4D perception and generation in a shared multimodal latent space, enabling a single model to perform zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Generative Pre-Training (GPT) represents a profound milestone in natural language processing, serving as the foundation for most of today's state-of-the-art large language models. By casting diverse understanding and generation problems into a shared token representation, GPT enables a single architecture and unified optimization loop to master heterogeneous tasks across massive corpora, giving rise to powerful foundation models for natural language. In contrast, 4D spatiotemporal vision lacks a common representational protocol: perception (such as depth and camera estimation) and generation are either handled by separate one-way pipelines or bolted together from unaligned single-modality spaces. In this work, we introduce WorldLatent, a framework that formulates a unified 4D latent space as a common protocol for both world perception and generation. Rather than relying on fragmented, task-specific models or stacked single-modality representations, our framework proposes a multimodal VAE architecture that compresses and fuses complex 4D visual appearance, geometry, and dynamics into a single structured latent representation. Within this formulation, perception and generation operate as complementary denoising processes over the same latent space, enabling one generative model to learn both capabilities simultaneously within a single training loop. Experiments across representative 4D benchmarks verify that our fused latent protocol achieves specialist-level zero-shot 4D perception, coherent 4D generation, and controllable editing from a single model. By demonstrating that diverse 4D tasks can be seamlessly unified under a shared representational protocol, our work provides a scalable foundation for general-purpose 4D spatial intelligence.

Abstract A hierarchical memory system using a vision-language model to select keyframes and an executor to act on them improves long-horizon robotic manipulation by retaining sparse visual details.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Robotic manipulation often requires remembering visual information that is no longer observable, such as the original location of an object. Existing approaches typically rely on full observation histories, which become redundant over long horizons, or text summaries, which may omit precise visual details. We study visual moment memory, the ability to retain such details from key moments for subsequent manipulation. To support this study, we introduce VMBench, a suite of long-horizon manipulation tasks requiring memory of spatial locations, visual appearances, and their associations. We further propose MomentMem, a hierarchical architecture in which a VLM-based memorizer selects keyframes and an executor predicts actions conditioned on the selected memories. This separation enables us to evaluate memory selection alongside closed-loop task success and to study memorizer generalization independently of the executor. On VMBench, MomentMem achieves 49.9% average success, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 31.7 percentage points. Moreover, we find that the memorizer transfers well to unseen appearances zero-shot. It can also adapt to an unseen task with only a few memory annotations, reaching 43.7% success, close to the 44.3% obtained with full memory supervision. Further experiments show competitive performance on RMBench and improvements in real-robot manipulation. These results highlight the value of retaining sparse visual evidence for memory-dependent manipulation. Our code is available at https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/MomentMem.

Abstract Hindsight Twins improves text-to-image alignment by distilling the velocity difference between twin prompt revisions while avoiding critic-induced bias.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Text-to-image models can learn from their own mistakes by distilling a self-teacher conditioned on a vision-language critic's revised prompt. However, such self-teachers barely improve alignment, and both they and their hindsight-contrast variants absorb the critic's unintended additions to the scene, such as lighting or scenery. We attribute this to two factors: (1) the prompt already specifies the desired fix, so a useful revision must go beyond the failed span, often rewriting the entire scene and adding details that overshadow the fix; (2) although the critic localizes the error to a single span, self-teachers condition on the rewrite, entangling the intended fix with unintended additions. Motivated by these insights, we propose Hindsight Twins (HinT), which fills the critic's revision template once with the intended fix and once with the observed error and distills only their velocity difference, anchored at the user's prompt. Because text encoders spread the edit of a single slot to all other tokens, HinT further aligns the twins' token states outside the slot. Across Qwen-Image-2512, SD3.5-M, and FLUX.2-klein, HinT keeps the rate of critic-induced bias at the base-model level while matching or exceeding the alignment gains of prior self-distillation, and it keeps improving over twelve rounds of self-training where contrastive baselines decay.

Abstract Adopting reasoning-oriented post-training recipes for knowledge tasks leads models to memorize self-generated hallucinations, especially on difficult questions, and makes them less likely to abstain from those false facts later.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Reasoning domains have shaped the post-training recipes of frontier models even though they are trained and deployed in domains beyond math and code. In this paper, we study the consequences of directly adopting reasoning-centric training recipes, e.g., the use of outcome-based rewards for reinforcement learning (RL), injection of "cognitive" behaviors such as proposing and backtracking from hypotheses in reasoning chains, etc., for knowledge tasks. We hypothesize that these decisions, while useful for reasoning, inadvertently encourage models to memorize hallucinations they themselves generate in their long reasoning chains when trained on knowledge tasks. To study this, we train models with standard post-training, extract their self-generated hallucinations during RL, and track memorization throughout the rest of training. Our experiments on six open models across two model families and two architectures confirm our hypothesis. We find that models' recall of hallucinations reinforced during training increases by 4.83% on average, compared to just 0.17% for self-hallucinations that receive no training signal. In fact, models even memorize false facts that they immediately retract in their reasoning; a backtracking template inherited from reasoning-centric training that has no benefit for knowledge recall. Furthermore, we find that memorization is strongest on the hardest training questions, and rewarding a hallucination makes the model less likely to abstain from it later (by 7.23% on average). Overall, our work identifies and systematically studies a previously unexamined pathway through which models learn hallucinations during training.

Abstract  Most LLM agent frameworks build a crew that runs a task and exits. Physical environments (e.g., homes, labs, factory floors) need agents that stay up for months, survive their own bugs, and run on whatever hardware is already next to the sensors. We present Wactorz, an open-source runtime in which LLM-generated agents are long-lived, supervised actors: a planner turns a chat request into Python, compiles it into a new actor at runtime, and a supervisor restarts that actor alone, with its state intact, when it crashes. All coordination rides on MQTT, so agents can be spawned on, and live-migrated between, edge nodes such as a Raspberry Pi, and every internal event is a topic external systems can subscribe to. The recorded demo builds a camera-to-lamp automation from one chat request, adds a second agent that is auto-wired to the first by topic and carries a deliberate division-by-zero, watches that agent repair its own code without losing its count, then deploys a fresh Raspberry Pi node and live-migrates the agent onto it, in under four minutes of wall-clock time and \$0.13 of model usage. This extended version adds what the two-page demonstration paper could not: the Wiring view of the topic graph, Wactorz as a library for hand-written agents, how trained models are stored, sent to nodes and replaced, the security model, and the system's limitations.

Abstract MORSE leverages recurring failures to search for adaptable methods and pretrained perception models, improving accuracy and speed of a frozen video-language model on streaming video question answering.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash A streaming video system must decide in a single pass what evidence to retain under bounded frame storage, before it knows which details a future question will require. Recent recursive self-improvement methods automate the design of the harness that stores and retrieves this evidence. They revise the harness in response to failures, but the editor must still devise each repair from its prior knowledge and a predefined set of tools. A failure that recurs across videos, however, may point to a problem that prior work has already addressed. We introduce MORSE (Memory Optimization with Recursive Self-Evolvement), an open-world auto-research framework that uses recurring failures to guide an online search for solutions. Its editor groups failures in training traces by cause, then searches papers, model cards, and code for methods it can adapt or pretrained perception models it can use. When the editor introduces a new perceptual capability, it can also revise how the harness stores and retrieves the resulting evidence for a frozen video-language model. On held-out validation streams, MORSE first selects revisions that improve answer quality, then seeks faster revisions that preserve those gains within a specified tolerance. Across three streaming video benchmarks, MORSE consistently achieves the highest answer accuracy within each frozen-backbone comparison. On OVO-Bench, our Qwen3-VL-8B harness answers queries in 0.30 seconds on average while achieving 75.1% accuracy, 3.1 percentage points ahead of the next-best evaluated harness using the same frozen backbone.

Abstract Feedback Shortcut reduces speculative decoding errors caused by feeding top-layer hidden states back into Transformer inputs by aligning projected states and correcting drafts with predecessor states, preserving exact-decoding quality while accelerating decoding.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Feeding each position's top-layer hidden state back into the next position's input improves Transformer performance. However, this feedback breaks speculative decoding, since verifying a drafted token requires the top-layer state from its preceding position, which forces the draft to rely on an approximate stand-in. Training the model to adapt to this stand-in can lower the exact-decoding performance ceiling, while using it at inference time further introduces approximation error. We propose Feedback Shortcut to address both bottlenecks. First, state alignment trains a lightweight projection from multi-token prediction head states to stand-ins, leaving base model training untouched. Second, pipelined correction re-evaluates each draft using its predecessor's newly computed state, a much closer proxy to true feedback. Running this correction inside the verification pass of subsequent drafts improves accuracy with zero extra calls. Across 135M and 780M scales, Feedback Shortcut stays within 10^{-4} bits per byte of the exact-decoding ceiling on Paloma and accelerates decoding by 2.4-2.8x with about 2% more training compute than the latent feedback Transformer. At large batch sizes, skipping correction still preserves Transformer speed and over 80% of latent feedback's gains.

Abstract PersistWorld maintains a scene-anchored 3D world state updated from camera streams and forecasts future semantic occupancy more accurately and faster than prior camera-based world models.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash 4D occupancy forecasting predicts the 3D semantic occupancy of a driving scene seconds ahead. Over a few seconds, the scene changes little while the vehicle moves, so most of the space ahead has already been seen. Sorting the future into unchanged, changed, and unseen space, we find that current camera-based world models fall short on the unchanged part: they fail to hold on to what they have already seen, rather than to forecast what will change. We therefore make what the model has seen its state. PersistWorld streams camera frames into a persistent 3D world state anchored in the scene, writing each frame once instead of rebuilding the state from past frames. The state is learned end to end with the forecast, and an evolver predicts it at any queried time along the ego trajectory. On Occ3D-nuScenes, PersistWorld raises the IoU of the best camera-based world model by 19 points at 1-3 s and by 18 to 24 points up to 8 s, while running more than twice as fast. Code and models: https://github.com/wangzian828/PersistWorld. Project page: https://wangzian828.github.io/PersistWorld

Abstract A benchmarking pipeline evaluates coding agents on optimizing CPU kernels across evolving Arm architectural extensions, showing strong gains on established SIMD extensions but unreliable adaptation to newer matrix-tile programming models.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Efficient AI computation depends on hardware that evolves over time, shifting the strategies needed to implement performant code. For CPU kernels, a key evolving factor is the set of available architectural extensions, which expose specialized hardware instructions that change the techniques used to optimize kernels. We study how effectively coding agents use these new, potentially unfamiliar extensions to write fast code. We introduce KernelShift, a benchmarking pipeline and a set of environments for evaluating coding agents in kernel optimization across evolving CPU architectures. Its agent-assisted, human-verified pipeline turns code repositories into benchmark instances, and its environments let agents optimize code with feedback from the target hardware. Using this pipeline, we construct 99 kernels from real-world, actively developed repositories (ncnn, llama.cpp, Arm SIMD Loops, and KleidiAI), including inference workloads derived from real model executions. KernelShift supports incorporating new kernels and hardware configurations as libraries and processors evolve. We evaluate agents with proprietary and open-weight models across progressively newer architectural extensions (Arm Neon, SVE, SVE2, and SME2), examining how performance changes as hardware evolves. On established extensions, agents beat the auto-vectorized compiler everywhere (2.4-19.8x) and exceed general-purpose libraries such as ncnn (getting up to 2.74x over its Neon kernels), but fall behind Arm's hand-tuned KleidiAI kernels (0.70x). In end-to-end inference of two LLMs, agent-written kernels achieve a 1.3-1.6x prefill speedup over llama.cpp's fast path. However, current agents do not yet reliably adapt to new extensions that require unfamiliar programming models, such as SME2's matrix tiles.

Abstract A unified power-law scoring rule for cache eviction calibrates one exponent per model and yields a method that improves language-model perplexity and preserves question-answering accuracy without dataset-specific tuning.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash The decay exponent α in KV-cache eviction is an intrinsic property of the model: calibrated once on a single corpus, it transfers directly across domains. Attention-based accumulation (H2O) and recency-based sliding windows (StreamingLLM) are two dominant eviction frameworks, often treated as unrelated, but we show that they are the two endpoints of a single power-law family: by scoring each token as s_i = A_i cdot (t-i)^{-α}, where A_i is cumulative attention and α controls the age penalty, H2O and StreamingLLM correspond to α=0 and αtoinfty, respectively (with StreamingLLM additionally hard-coding an attention-sink patch).   The no-hair mechanism of xiang2026nohair bounds the admissible range of α, and the measured exponents agree: calibrating once on WikiText yields [0.556, 0.822] for a representative model (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct). The per-model ranges are consistent across five models (410M--1.5B) and independently match the optimal regions from perplexity sweeps.   NoHairCache achieves the lowest perplexity across three datasets (WikiText, PG19, and arXiv) without per-dataset tuning, outperforming both H2O and StreamingLLM (p < 0.05 on all three datasets, with 0.06--0.10 PPL gains over StreamingLLM at budget 512). On downstream four-way multiple-choice QA, it matches or outperforms StreamingLLM across all nine (task, budget) cells, retaining 98.2% of full-cache accuracy on NarrativeQA at 25% retention versus 91.1% for StreamingLLM; per-cell McNemar tests are not significant, so QA is corroborating only. We release our calibration script and drop-in scorer upon publication.

Abstract  Graphical user interface (GUI) agents are widely trained in executable GUI environments. To lower cost, build environments in batches, and reduce manual effort, each work trades away part of the environment through its construction choices. We describe these choices along three dimensions: reward coverage, the goal-reaching trajectories the verifier rewards; data coverage, the application data the agent is exposed to beyond what the task reads or writes; and feature coverage, the application features beyond what the task uses. Existing works set each dimension on engineering grounds, but they do not measure its effect on training. How complete must an environment be to train a capable agent, and along which dimensions? In MobileGym, we adjust each dimension separately, train Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct, and evaluate every policy in the original full environment. A verifier that rewards only one reference path lowers success by 10.53 points, and removing unneeded records also lowers success. Pruning unneeded features instead raises success from 51.61% to 56.13%, entirely on tasks whose environments it left unchanged. The gain reverses when each episode is weighted equally and vanishes when only episodes on the pruned tasks are balanced. Without pruning, length weighting there reinforces long detour successes that erode skills on other tasks. Following these findings, we build MobileGym-Lite, which keeps reward and data coverage complete and prunes unneeded features. It outperforms Full in the full environment (55.48% versus 51.61% success) with lower construction cost.

Abstract Fast-JEPA is a latent world model planning framework that improves the success-latency trade-off of predictive search by predicting complete action-sequence outcomes and using goal-directed plans, with evaluation on manipulation and navigation tasks and a new dynamic planning benchmark. Abstract GlanceNav is a streaming multimodal navigator that maintains constant per-step memory through recurrent and attention mechanisms and uses a reinforcement learning correction method to improve vision-and-language navigation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.

Abstract Mutual Information Prototype Alignment distills datasets for self-supervised learning by optimizing synthetic prototypes to preserve augmentation-invariant information and improve learned representations.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Dataset distillation (DD) aims to compress a large dataset into a small synthetic one that remains effective for training neural networks. Despite its success in supervised learning, DD remains underexplored in self-supervised learning (SSL), where distilled data must preserve the information needed to learn representations that remain consistent across augmentations. To bridge this gap, we introduce Mutual Information Prototype Alignment (MIPA), an efficient SSL dataset distillation approach that optimizes synthetic images to preserve augmentation-invariant information. Specifically, MIPA treats synthetic images as learnable prototypes and uses a frozen encoder to assign real samples to them based on feature similarity. A synthetic dataset that preserves such augmentation invariance should yield stable prototype assignments across multiple augmented views of the same real image. MIPA encourages this by maximizing mutual information between cross-view assignments while avoiding trivial solutions in which all real samples are assigned to only a few synthetic prototypes. Evaluations on image classification benchmarks show that synthetic data generated by MIPA yield higher-quality representations than existing distillation approaches while reducing time and storage demands during distillation.

Abstract  Language model agents are increasingly asked to run ongoing multi-day operations rather than finish isolated single-turn tasks. In daily operations, today's choices reshape later observations and cash constraints because empty shelves hide lost walk-in demand, short-shipped customers quietly shrink future orders weeks later, and emergency buying drains working capital. We introduce SMB Bench, a calibrated 90-day wholesale simulator with 18 causally paired tasks and an 859-run frontier trace dataset, to ask what experience and learning signal make an agent better at noticing, attributing, and acting on the delayed consequences of its own decisions in unseen businesses. Across 16 frontier models, only 3 outperform a fixed buying rule; models often record a relevant diagnosis in their notes without changing later purchases in time, showing that diagnosing a problem, changing behavior, and improving a business are separate achievements. To study how experience improves those failures in an open model (Qwen3.8-27B), we used training-business records to identify operational rules and archived frontier quarters as starting states to continue from rather than transcripts to copy. On an unseen test business under identical evaluation instructions, the trained policy followed customer-protection commitments more consistently and reduced severe-loss quarters, while the additional profit benefit remained uncertain. Carrying those commitments through the quarter exposed the cost of fulfilling them, as early-quarter stocking habits left the buyer relying on emergency supply and a deteriorating supplier. The results separate recognizing a delayed effect, acting on it consistently, and coordinating surrounding decisions so that the response improves profit. SMB Bench connects these observations to a learning method by turning archived decisions and their consequences into reusable training experience.

Abstract ReWeave jointly optimizes an embodied agent harness and an emergent physical task topology from interaction without updating policy weights, improving success and enabling continual learning across tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Long-horizon embodied tasks involve physical dependencies that may only become clear through interaction. As task understanding deepens, past trials can reveal new information for adaptation and transfer. However, coarse progress feedback cannot identify which physical preconditions held, leaving search unguided, while task-specific optimization easily overfits to individual scenes, making cross-task transfer brittle. We introduce ReWeave, a framework that jointly optimizes an embodied agent harness and an emergent physical task topology from interaction without updating policy weights. Keeping execution policies and perception frozen, ReWeave co-optimizes coupled context, workflows, and control loops. As new evidence revises the topology, ReWeave reprojects past trials onto the updated task understanding, exposing retained progress, regressions, and bottlenecks that guide parent selection and search width. Across tasks, matching verified local mechanisms to unmet preconditions consolidates 72 specialist units into 44 reusable groups (0.34times full concatenation) within a compact shared harness capable of continual learning. ReWeave improves held-out success from 89.00% to 98.75% on LIBERO-PRO and from 25.50% to 58.25% on RoboDojo, while uncovering retained progress in 89% of 159 failed RoboDojo development trials and translating stronger offline reasoning into higher online performance. On eight unseen LIBERO-PRO tasks, the shared harness reaches 51.3% success versus 41.3% for direct distillation, rising to 75.0% after continual learning.

Abstract  Vision-language-action (VLA) models leverage pretrained vision-language representations for instruction-conditioned robot control, yet their understanding of task-relevant 3D relations and how these relations evolve during manipulation remains largely implicit. To address this challenge, we introduce WeaveVLA, a framework that enhances VLA action generation by explicitly modeling task-relevant relative 3D relations and maintaining relational context across observations. WeaveVLA employs a task-conditioned sparse graph neural network to encode relative 3D relations among objects, the gripper, and nearby references. Pair-specific recurrent edge states integrate geometric evidence across observations to maintain evolving spatial context. A learned-query readout summarizes the updated graph representations into compact relational tokens, which condition the action expert alongside the original vision-language context. An auxiliary geometry reconstruction objective further encourages spatial structure preservation. We instantiate WeaveVLA on StarVLA-GR00T and π_{0.5}, achieving over 98\% average success on LIBERO with both backbones. On LIBERO-Plus, WeaveVLA improves success from 74.9\% to 82.0\% and from 85.7\% to 87.8\%, respectively, with additional improvements on CALVIN DrightarrowD. On three real-world tasks, the StarVLA-GR00T-based variant improves average success from 55.6\% to 70.0\% under standard conditions and from 33.3\% to 53.3\% under OOD perturbations, surpassing the π_{0.5} baseline in both settings.

Abstract An attention-based process reward model that rewards internal reasoning through hidden states and attention maps improves reinforcement learning for large language models, yielding an average pass@8 gain of 2.88%.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Reinforcement learning (RL) has become an important component in large language models (LLMs). However, existing RL approaches focus on final outputs or textual solution trajectories, leading to coarse-grained rewards and unreliable reasoning. Such paradigms focus mainly on what the model outputs while overlooking how the model internally reasons. Therefore, can we reward how a model thinks internally rather than merely what it says? We first reveal the problem of textual reward aliasing in existing reward modeling approaches. Motivated by this, we propose the Attention Process Reward Model (APRM), which assigns internal rewards based on hidden states and attention maps to capture high-quality reasoning patterns. With only approximately 46M parameters, APRM is trained using ranking and counterfactual objectives, and its reward guides RL optimization while being periodically updated to handle distribution shifts. Integrated with representative RL methods, APRM achieved an average pass@8 gain of 2.88\%. Our results demonstrate the promise of incorporating internal reasoning signals into RL for LLMs.

Abstract A task-aware multimodal memory framework consolidates experience from completed tasks using source-linked multi-view memory, source-generated tasks, distillation, and task-aware retrieval, yielding consistent gains across memory benchmarks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash AI agents accumulate conversations, images, and documents through long-term interactions with users. Answering later questions often requires connecting evidence across these records, yet the resulting understanding is rarely retained, allowing mistakes to recur on related questions. Existing multimodal memory methods largely focus on storing and retrieving records, with limited mechanisms for learning from completed tasks and consolidating task-specific experience. We therefore propose MMMem, a task-aware multimodal memory framework that extracts and consolidates experience from completed tasks through four complementary strategies. First, source-linked multi-view memory organizes original text, images, and rendered pages alongside generated scene images that combine clues across records for retrieval. Second, source-generated tasks support knowledge alignment at two levels: warm-up consolidates source knowledge into task-relevant experiences in external memory, while self-training adapts the model's parametric knowledge to task demands. Third, the same vision-language model distills completed tasks into experiences that record the task context, conclusion, and support links. Finally, task-aware retrieval recalls source views and relevant experiences for answering and verification, while source-supported reuse guides long-term retention. Across three memory benchmarks and two vision-language readers, MMMem outperforms eight baselines. With Qwen3.5-9B, MMMem achieves relative gains of 36.9%, 17.6%, and 15.2% over the best-performing baselines on LoCoMo, Mem-Gallery, and LooGLE v2, respectively. Ablations show consistent gains from training and memory warm-up, while case studies illustrate how individual components work in practice.

Abstract  Optimized floating-point inference is not reproducible across environments: the same model on different hardware, compilers or thread counts can produce different bits. We present tritify, an open-source runtime that executes the forward pass of real models -- decoder-only language models, Mixture-of-Experts decoders, and the Segment Anything 2.1 image/video stack -- with the entire run-time path in exact integer arithmetic; floating point is confined to offline quantization and the float reference used for verification. We make three separate claims and verify each. (1) Cross-hardware reproducibility: the integer path produces bit-identical output run to run, across CPU instruction-set architectures, and across CPU/GPU, carried by a reproducing SHA-256 of the output. (2) Decision fidelity: the integer path reproduces the float reference's decision on the models and inputs tested, with the numeric exceptions measured; per-layer fidelity through sixteen layers of OLMoE-1B-7B and of a 106-billion-parameter MoE shows no fidelity cliff. (3) Location independence: a model's computation can be split across instruction sets, CPU and GPU, and networked machines with zero effect on the output bits. GLM-4.5-Air (106,852,251,264 parameters, 427 GB of integer codes) generated its answer across three machines, none of which can hold its integer codes in RAM, bit-identically to a single-host reference. The consequence is verifiable distributed inference: an untrusted server or a fleet of devices runs the model while an open scalar oracle re-derives the exact bytes; a guarantee against a dishonest server is future work. We do not claim a per-device speed-up, bit-equality of floating-valued intermediates, or confidentiality. Code: https://github.com/pelloran-inc/tritify

Abstract  Hierarchical text detection aims to localize words, text lines, and paragraphs while parsing their structural relationships. It provides essential structural cues for visual text understanding. To maximize accuracy, existing methods typically rely on complex multi-scale feature fusion necks and heavy-weight heads, resulting in bloated architectures and low inference efficiency. In this paper, we argue that a sufficiently pre-trained backbone can effectively replace these sophisticated external modules, while simple geometric inclusion suffices for hierarchical text structural parsing. Thus, we propose Prism, a minimalist framework for hierarchical text detection. Prism performs multi-granularity detection by attaching parallel lightweight heads to a single-scale backbone feature map, covering words, text lines, and paragraphs, and reconstructs the structural hierarchy via an efficient geometric inclusion algorithm. In addition, we systematically compare representative lightweight head archetypes and show that pixel-level regression offers the best accuracy-efficiency trade-off in this setting. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks show that our significantly simplified architecture achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance with remarkable efficiency. Specifically, it runs 1.7times faster than LRANet on CTW1500 while achieving higher accuracy, and surpasses the current SOTA method, OmniParser V2, by over 6 PQ points on the hierarchical HierText dataset. Code will be released soon.

Abstract  Asking a vision-language model (VLM) to think step by step often makes it worse at spatial questions, even though the image stays in context. We show that short chain-of-thought (CoT) turns a perception problem into a transcription problem. Once a VLM writes a spatial relation, its answer follows that sentence instead of the image, so whether CoT helps or hurts depends largely on whether the written relation is more accurate than answering directly. Studying 15 open VLMs (2B-38B) and six frontier closed models, we rule out token count, turn format, and visual forgetting in open models and trace the failure through three stages. First, writing re-weights visual cues, weakening a "lower is closer" shortcut in all 13 open models tested but also discarding valid cues, which produces false relations. Second, the written text takes over. With the model's own conclusion-removed rationale held fixed, swapping in an opposite-depth image moves the answer by 2-9 points on rendered twins (vs. up to 77 for direct answers) and by 3.0-3.4 points on average on natural photographs (vs. 20.5-69.6). Third, the concluding sentence can invert even a true relation, mainly on converse questions. Together, false relations and conclusion mis-mapping account for four in five CoT losses. Scale does not remove this bottleneck: Qwen3-VL-32B shows the same pattern. Frontier closed models are not hurt: they write relations as accurately as they answer, and three of six re-check the image after writing. The three queried with reasoning off lock in like open models, so re-grounding co-occurs with answer-turn reasoning; we identify answer-step re-grounding as the step open VLMs lack. In a single-model pilot, fine-tuning on fixed rationales with image-determined labels restores it on natural photographs. We open-sourced all our datasets and code at https://ming-zch.github.io/seeing-right-writing-wrong.

Abstract  Typhoon OCR 1.5 focused on reading Thai documents. However, in real-world workflows, users often need to extract specific information or preserve the structure of an entire page for further processing. Typhoon OCR 2 handles both tasks in a single model, with prompt-driven extraction and structured full-page parsing. Full-page parsing covers Thai, English, and Chinese. We release compact 2B and 0.8B models adapted from open document parsers through supervised fine-tuning on curated Thai and public multilingual data, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with task rewards and a rule penalizing unfinished responses. Among evaluated open models, Typhoon OCR 2 2B scores highest on Thai MDPBench (80.7 versus 68.9 for Typhoon OCR 1.5) and on ThaiOCRBench (59.91 versus 40.45), which covers recognition, extraction, and document understanding. The 2B model remains close to its base on English olmOCR-bench (84.7 versus 84.4) and on OmniDocBench, which evaluates English and Chinese together (90.97 versus 91.03). On MDPBench, GRPO reduces the mean share of pages with repetition loops for the 2B model from 3.2% to 0.7%, with a smaller and statistically uncertain score gain for the 0.8B model. We release the model weights, Python package, and evaluation suite.

Abstract Paired non-contrast and contrast-enhanced medical images reveal that visual content retrieved through value vectors, rather than spatial attention alone, is critical for multimodal model perception, motivating a distillation method that improves non-contrast inference.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Current MLLM methods largely focus on whether models attend to the right visual regions, implicitly treating spatial attention map as the primary key of effective visual perception. To examine this assumption, we introduce a medical VQA dataset with paired non-contrast (NC) and contrast-enhanced (CE) images that share corresponding anatomy while providing different cues. We show that this view is incomplete: across paired NC and CE medical images, MLLMs often exhibit similar attention patterns despite producing contrasting answers, while controlled bidirectional interventions induce substantially larger prediction changes on visual values than attention weights. These results identify the visual content retrieved through values as a critical factor in improving perception beyond spatial attention alone. Guided by this finding, we propose VIVID (VIsual Value Intervention Distillation), which uses enhanced visual values to identify and refine teacher corrections aligned with useful visual evidence to improve on-policy distillation from CE to NC images. Across six internal and external test sets, VIVID consistently outperforms existing on-policy distillation methods across different model scales and architectures, requires only NC images at inference, and maintains strong zero-shot performance on general-domain VQA benchmarks. The project page is available at https://qiuzyc.github.io/VIVID.

Abstract Frontier models can identify whether text was written by an AI or human, often which lab or exact author, threatening anonymity for prolific online writers.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Most strikingly, Fable 5.2 correctly names the author of 55% of 15K anonymous ICML 2020 reviews that were never made public, and identifies the username behind 69% of comments from active Redditors (with >500 posts over the last year). Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.

Abstract  Agentic systems are increasingly being explored for automating experimental high-energy physics measurements, but the correctness of their results can today be reliably evaluated only via extensive expert review. To address this, we propose a method to perform detailed quantitative evaluations of agent-driven analyses through the use of blind analysis and injection tests, which are critical in a typical expert review. Agents develop a data analysis pipeline on a public dataset, while the evaluation is performed on programmatically generated sealed private datasets where signals are injected and simulation settings varied within realistic detector uncertainties. By requiring agents to provide a single workflow that is evaluated across these "sealed worlds", we are able to test the accuracy and robustness of the workflows. We demonstrate the approach on four tasks built from CMS open data and present it as a way to reliably benchmark scientific work in experimental high-energy physics. At the time of writing, the best-performing model, Gemini 4 Argon, reaches 60% of the score of the expert reference analyses on average, ahead of GPT 6 Astra at 38% and Claude Opus 5.5 at 31%. In individual sessions on one of the tasks, Gemini 4 Argon exceeds the reference. The benchmark is hosted at https://hep-ex-bench.github.io

Abstract Lyris uses persistent point-cloud memory and vision-language-model-authored 3D layouts to generate long, camera-controlled walkthroughs from a single photo, improving camera accuracy and return consistency.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Exploring a scene from as little as a single photo requires generating a long, camera-controlled walkthrough that extends far beyond what was captured, yet remains consistent upon return. Memory-based video generation methods preserve what they have already seen, but have no anchor beyond it, where the camera drifts from its path and a text prompt can name objects but not place them. Layout-conditioned methods provide such an anchor and precise control over what appears where, but rely on layouts that are typically authored by hand. Both limitations arise because, beyond what was captured, nothing anchors the video model unless a human authors one. To overcome both, we propose Lyris, a long video generation framework that never leaves the video model without an anchor: a persistent point-cloud memory where the scene has been observed or generated, and a 3D layout that a vision-language model authors where it has not. Our key insight is that the interface between the two models can be surprisingly coarse. A layout of one labeled 3D box per object is simple enough for a vision-language model to author (and for a user to edit), yet expressive enough for a video model to follow once rendered along the camera path. We demonstrate walkthroughs of over three minutes and 50 meters from a single photo, passing through doorways into rooms it never shows. When evaluated on minute-long round trips across four datasets, Lyris outperforms state-of-the-art methods by a wide margin, reducing camera error by 3.8x and improving consistency upon return by 22% on average.

Abstract A simulation benchmark shows that robot manipulation policies often fail at non-average conveyor speeds, while past frames or speed inputs improve models when speed is linearly decodable.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Robot policies must adapt to the speed of moving objects, yet existing benchmarks rarely measure this ability across diverse tasks and speeds. We introduce SWEEP-Bench, a simulation benchmark with 80 conveyor-belt manipulation tasks spanning 16 skill categories, each evaluated at 13 belt speeds (0.10-0.40 m/s). Objects are transported through physical contact with a visibly moving belt. Each task has a static counterpart, and each evaluation episode uses the same randomized scene at all 13 speeds. We fine-tune eight vision-language-action and world-action models on demonstrations spanning the full speed range, comparing observations from the current frame alone with those including two past frames. For six models, the success rate peaks at intermediate speeds, and their failures indicate that they tend to produce actions suited to the average demonstration speed rather than to the actual one. Past frames raise the success rate by 10-40 percentage points for three models, and linear probes show that the belt speed is also most linearly decodable from these three. Adding ground-truth speed input to four of the models improves all of them, and a speed estimated from the images keeps most of this gain. These results show that SWEEP-Bench exposes speed-dependent failures and suggest that past frames help only policies from which the speed is most decodable, such as those pretrained on multiple frames. Project page: https://damin3927.github.io/SWEEP-Bench

Abstract CCFinance reconstructs firm coverage from public news archives under different selection rules, using model-assisted labeling and validation, and shows how mention thresholds affect issuer-year counts and financial network measures.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Proprietary news archives assign stories to firms, so reproducing a financial study often requires access to both the articles and the vendor's assignments. CCFinance lets researchers reconstruct firm coverage under different selection rules by recording which version of a story was captured, which issuers it names and how often each appears. From 14.8 million English-language records in Fine News, derived from public Common Crawl News, we identify 5.22 million distinct, consistently dated articles with issuer links. Exact reconstruction requires the restricted snapshot and derived artifacts. We compare GLiNER and Stanza on 250 model-labeled article leads and assess issuer links and story focus against blinded model judgments. Estimated precision among 475 accepted links is 96.8%. Among held-out articles with a detected company, requiring three mentions raises subject precision from 31.9% to 66.1%. Across the 2017-2025 panel, the same rule reduces the number of issuer-years with at least 50 qualifying stories from 13,918 to 8,641. Three reimplementations examine volatility tracking, bank networks and overnight returns. In the network study, bank pairs appearing together rose from 177 to 242 across publishers between January and April 2020, but fell from 50 to 35 within Reuters.

Abstract  Touchard and the author asked whether every valued hyperfield, in Krasner's sense with the norm axiom, is of the form H_ρ(K): a valued field K read up to relative error ρ. The question turns on the norm axiom. If a valued hyperfield is a quotient K^times/Tcup{0} of a field, the norm axiom forces T=1+m_ρ for a valuation on K. This completes the proof of a published proposition, and it makes the question equivalent to asking whether every Krasner valued hyperfield is such a quotient. For value group Z we prove directly that it is. Without the norm axiom the answer is negative. Layering a hyperfield over a non-trivial ordered abelian group gives a factor hyperfield exactly when the hyperfield itself is one, so Massouros's hyperfields yield hyperfields with a non-trivial valuation, satisfying all the other axioms, that are not factor hyperfields. The general question remains open.

Abstract UniVEP combines endpoint and velocity prediction with consistency supervision and switches from endpoint recovery to velocity refinement as noise decreases, improving success and efficiency in few-step robot action generation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Flow-matching robot policies usually use one prediction target throughout action generation, even though recovering actions from noise and polishing nearly clean actions bring different challenges. We systematically compare velocity prediction (V), endpoint prediction (X), and velocity-weighted endpoint prediction (X--V) across multiple robot policies, benchmarks, and inference budgets. On StarVLA and LiLa-WAM, X--V works best for few-step generation, while V gains more from iterative refinement. We further uncover their complementary strengths across noise levels: clean actions have concentrated covariance spectra, endpoint predictions stay more stable under high noise, and velocity predictions offer more accurate corrections near clean actions. Inspired by these findings, we introduce UniVEP, which learns both prediction targets together with consistency supervision and switches from endpoint recovery to velocity refinement as noise drops. Each step only needs one network run, and joint training also boosts single-step endpoint generation. Tests on simulated and real-world manipulation show better success rates and efficiency. On RoboTwin, UniVEP hits 61.36% success with one StarVLA run, beating the best single-target baseline at sixteen runs (59.96%). With LiLa-WAM, two runs outperform the best sixteen-step single-target baseline and cut inference latency by 77.1%.
